And, you know, honestly? Looking back, yeah, this was definitely around when I was a kid, too. It's not really a new thing, it just looks different now because I'm an adult running a business instead of a kid in a classroom. \n\nBut back in elementary and middle school? Oh, man, it was a constant battle. I mean, my poor mother, she was always getting called in for parent-teacher conferences. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—sweetest lady, but I drove her absolutely crazy. She was always, and I mean always, moving my seat. First I'd be in the back, then I'd be up front right next to her desk, then I'd be off to the side by the window. She thought moving me would help, but the problem was I just talked too much. I'd talk to whoever was next to me, or I'd just start talking to the whole room if nobody was next to me. \n\nAnd the sitting still part? Forget about it. I was that kid who would just get up without asking. Like, we'd be in the middle of a math lesson, and I'd just stand up and walk over to the pencil sharpener, or go look at the class pet, whatever it was, just because I felt like I had to move. The teachers would get so frustrated. They'd say, \"You have to raise your hand,\" but by the time they said that, I was already halfway across the room. \n\nAnd the blurting things out... that was probably the worst of it. The teacher would be asking a question, like, halfway through the sentence, and I just couldn't hold it in. I knew the answer, or at least I thought I did, and I'd just shout it out before they even finished asking. I didn't mean to be disrespectful, I really didn't, it just felt like the words were exploding out of my mouth before my brain could even register that I was supposed to wait my turn. So, yeah, it's not just a recent thing. It's been there, just... simmering, I guess, since I was a little kid. It's just that now, it's messing with my livelihood and my marriage, not just my report card, you know?\n\nDoctor: Back in those school days, did these attention and restlessness problems cause you to actually fall behind academically, get into trouble, or have real consequences for you—or were you able to keep up with your schoolwork despite the fidgeting and talking out?\n\nPatient: Well, back in school... I mean, like I mentioned earlier, I didn't exactly stick around for graduation. I made it partway through high school and just... it was a wash. I couldn't do the whole \"sit at a desk and fill out worksheets\" thing, so I just left and started working. So, yeah, it definitely derailed me academically back then. But honestly, the part that's really weighing on me right now, the real consequences I'm dealing with today... it's a different kind of trouble. \n\nBecause, look, I'm still keeping up overall. The business is doing fine, we're in the black, I'm getting the core work done. I'm not failing at life, you know? But... it's the people. It's starting to really cost me trust at home, and I'm burning through the patience of people at work. \n\nLike, at home, my wife and I will have these family plans—weekend things, or organizing a get-together with her folks—and because my brain is just jumping all over the place or I can't sit through the planning part, things just don't go the way they should. I'll forget my part of it, or I'll be so restless during the actual event that she feels like I don't even want to be there. I can literally see the trust in her eyes slipping, like she's thinking she just can't count on me to be present. And that is a terrible feeling, it really is. \n\nAnd then at work, it's the exact same dynamic. I'll be in these strategy meetings, or worse, I'll be having these one-on-one mentoring conversations with my younger staff. I really want to help them grow, I genuinely care about their careers and passing on what I know, but I'll be sitting there in my office with them, and my leg is bouncing, I'm interrupting them, or I'm looking at the hallway every time someone walks by, and the conversation just... it doesn't go the way it should. They don't say anything because I'm the chief executive, right? They're not gonna chew out the boss. But I'm not stupid. I can see their patience wearing incredibly thin. I can see them shut down. So, yeah, I'm keeping up with the bottom line, but the collateral damage to my relationships every single day... it's just getting too heavy to keep ignoring.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year or so, have you had any periods where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or felt like you could accomplish way more than you normally do—like a few weeks where everything just felt possible and you were operating at a higher level than usual?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, honestly, as a guy running a business, you'd think I'd love to have a week where I just didn't need to sleep and could conquer the world, right? I read these articles about those tech guys who sleep three hours a night and build empires, and I always think, man, how do they actually do that? Because for me... no. I absolutely need my sleep. If I don't get my seven or eight hours, my wife will tell you, I am just a complete bear the next day. I'll be downing three, maybe four cups of coffee before 10 AM just to keep my eyes open. \n\nAnd as for feeling like I can accomplish way more than normal... it's really, it's usually the exact opposite. I have all this physical energy, like I told you about before, that motor running in my chest, but it's not a *productive* energy. It's not like I wake up on a Tuesday and suddenly I'm knocking out our quarterly projections and reorganizing the entire supply chain and feeling like Superman. It's more like... it's like a car stuck in neutral with the gas pedal slammed down. It makes a lot of noise and burns a lot of fuel, but it doesn't actually get you anywhere faster. \n\nSo by the end of the day, I'm actually exhausted just from trying to keep myself in the chair and keep myself focused. I definitely don't have these magical weeks where everything just feels possible and I don't need rest. I kind of wish I did, frankly, it would make dealing with the board of directors a lot easier! But no, my energy is just... it's just that constant, annoying, restless hum I've always had. It never really spikes up into some super-charged phase like you're talking about, it's just the same old everyday grind.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary or middle school, besides just not finishing high school, did you have any other significant consequences—like getting suspended, held back a grade, or being placed in special classes because of your behavior and attention problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because people always assume that if you end up dropping out of high school, you must have been this huge delinquent, right? Like you were getting suspended every other week for fighting or smoking behind the bleachers or something. But for me... no, honestly, none of that actually happened. I never got suspended. Not even once, if you can believe it. \n\nI mean, I spent a lot of time in the hallway. Teachers were always telling me, \"Just go stand outside until you can calm down,\" so I'd be out there pacing the tiles, counting the lockers, looking out the window. But they never actually sent me to the principal's office for a suspension or sent me home. I think they knew I wasn't trying to be bad or malicious, you know? I wasn't a mean kid. I was just... a lot to handle. Just constantly buzzing and talking out of turn. \n\nAnd as for being held back a grade or put in special classes, no, they didn't do that either. I think part of it was that I actually tested pretty well. Like, if you put a multiple-choice test in front of me, I could blast through it in ten minutes and get a B or a B-plus. It was the day-to-day stuff—the sitting still, the daily worksheets, the long reading assignments—that absolutely killed me. So my grades were always this really weird mix of acing the exams and getting straight zeros on the homework because I lost it, or forgot it, or just couldn't physically sit at the kitchen table long enough to finish it. \n\nBut it always averaged out to a C, you know? Just exactly enough to pass. So they just kept passing me along to the next grade. \"He's bright but lacks discipline,\" or \"needs to apply himself\"—that was the exact phrase, I swear it was stamped on every single report card I ever brought home. My mom had a whole stack of them in a drawer. They never put me in any special ed classes or anything like that. They just... I don't know, they just kind of tolerated me, moved my desk around like I mentioned before, and pushed me through the system until I finally just took myself out of the equation in high school. So yeah, no official punishments, no held back grades, no special programs. Just a whole lot of exasperated sighs from the teachers and scraping by right in the middle of the pack.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did your teachers or parents ever mention that your attention problems or restlessness were affecting your ability to make friends, or did other kids seem to notice and react to your fidgeting and talking out of turn?\n\nPatient: You know, honestly, no. That’s one area where I never really struggled back then. Making friends was actually pretty easy for me. I think when you’re a kid, especially a boy in elementary or middle school, having a ton of energy and always wanting to do something doesn’t exactly make you a pariah, you know? If anything, it kind of worked in my favor socially. I was always the one ready to go play wall-ball, or organize a game of pickup basketball, or just, you know, goof around in the back of the bus. \n\nThe other kids didn’t care if my leg was bouncing a million miles an hour under the cafeteria table. They definitely didn't care if I talked out of turn in class—honestly, half the time they probably thought it was funny when I blurted something out and derailed the teacher's lesson. It made me a bit of a class clown, I guess, or at least the guy who kept things from getting boring. My parents and teachers, they never once pulled me aside to say, 'Hey, your fidgeting is alienating your peers' or 'You need to calm down so kids will like you.' Their whole focus, one hundred percent of the time, was just on getting me to sit still long enough to finish a math worksheet. \n\nSo socially, I always had a really solid group of guys I hung out with. We’d ride our bikes all over the neighborhood, build ramps, run around until the streetlights came on... and my restlessness just sort of blended right into all that childhood chaos. It’s actually kind of ironic when you think about it, right? The exact same behavior that makes you an absolute nightmare for a fourth-grade teacher trying to get through a reading lesson is exactly what makes you a fun kid to hang out with at recess. \n\nIt wasn't until I got older, and especially now in the business world like I was saying before, that the talking over people and the constant moving started actually bothering people and causing friction. Because adults expect you to sit in a boardroom and be perfectly polished. But back then? No, not at all. The other kids never batted an eye at it, and it never cost me any friendships.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you tested well on exams but struggled with homework and daily assignments. Looking back, do you think your attention and restlessness problems actually prevented you from learning material, or was it more that you just didn't complete the work even though you could have understood it if you'd sat down and done it?\n\nPatient: That is a really interesting question. You know, I’ve actually thought about that a lot over the years, especially after I started the business and suddenly had to learn all these complicated things on the fly—like tax codes and supply chain logistics and all that. And looking back at high school... I honestly don't really know for sure. I don't have a clear-cut answer for you, because I don't really know what it feels like to sit down and deeply study something, so I don't know what I actually missed out on. \n\nI mean, I never felt stupid, you know? I never sat in a class and thought, \"Wow, this material is just way over my head, I can't grasp these concepts.\" If a teacher was standing up at the chalkboard explaining history or science, I was absorbing it. I might have been completely dismantling a clicky pen at my desk, or tapping my foot so hard the kid next to me was getting annoyed, or staring out the window at the parking lot, but I was still hearing the words. And that’s exactly how I could pass those multiple-choice tests I mentioned. I would just sort of recall what Mr. Davis said on a Tuesday while I was taking apart my pen. \n\nBut as far as sitting down with the homework to see if I truly understood it? It’s like... I couldn't even get far enough into the process to find out if I could learn it that way. Doing the homework was just a complete non-starter. My mom, bless her, she used to try everything. She would set this little plastic tomato kitchen timer and say, \"Just sit here at the table for twenty minutes. Just twenty minutes, read the biology chapter, and then you can go outside.\" And I would sit there, and I would stare at the page, and I could physically read the words, but my brain was already in the backyard, or thinking about building a bike ramp, or just literally buzzing with this overwhelming physical need to stand up. It was like the bridge between my brain understanding a concept and my hand actually writing it down on a worksheet was just completely broken. \n\nSo, did the restlessness prevent me from learning the material? Maybe? I guess it depends on how you define learning. I probably missed out on a lot of the deeper details because I never did the reading. But I always felt like, if I could have just magically downloaded the information into my head while I was walking around or doing something active, I would have understood it perfectly fine. Even today, if my lawyers hand me a fifty-page contract to review, I can't just sit in my leather chair and read it line by line. I’ll pace the entire length of the office, or I'll have my assistant break it down into bullet points and read them out loud to me while I'm pacing, because if I try to just sit still and read it, my eyes glaze over, my leg starts bouncing, and I am just... I'm done. The tank is empty. So I guess, to answer your question, I really don't know if I could have learned it better back then. I just know that the physical act of sitting still to do the daily work was basically impossible for me, regardless of whether I understood the subject or not.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you dropped out of high school and that you've always struggled with sitting still and focusing. First through fifth grade. Yeah, I think it was definitely way more on the \"annoying to deal with\" side of things, honestly. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about my grades and the testing, they weren't really worried that I wasn't grasping the concepts, because I could spit the answers right back at them. The problem was *how* I was spitting the answers back. Looking back, this was definitely around when I was a kid too—teachers were just always, constantly moving my seat. It was like this never-ending game of musical chairs, but I was the only one playing. \n\nI’d start the year in the middle row, right? But then I talked too much to whoever was sitting next to me, so the teacher would move me to the front corner to keep an eye on me. But then I’d be right in their line of sight, and I was always getting up without asking—just standing up and wandering over to the pencil sharpener, or going to look out the window, or walking over to check on the class hamster right in the middle of a spelling lesson. So then they’d get fed up with me pacing around the front of the room and they'd move my desk to the very back. \n\nBut putting me in the back didn't stop me, because then I'd just be blurting things out before they even finished the question. A teacher would be halfway through setting up a math problem on the chalkboard and I'd just shout out \"seventy-two!\" from the back row. I just couldn't hold it in. My brain was moving fast, I knew the answer, so why wait for her to finish talking, you know? \n\nSo when it came to parent-teacher conferences, it was never really about my learning or my academic potential. My mom would come home, sit at the kitchen table, let out this huge sigh, and tell me the teacher just spent twenty minutes complaining about how I couldn't stay in my chair and how I wouldn't stop talking over people. They were just exasperated. I think they just found me exhausting to manage. The whole focus was just trying to figure out where to put my desk so I'd stop driving everyone crazy, rather than worrying about whether I was actually absorbing the geography lesson.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Just a whole lot of exasperated sighs from the teachers and scraping by right in the middle of the pack.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school, did your teachers or parents ever mention that your attention problems or restlessness were affecting your ability to make friends, or did other kids seem to notice and react to your fidgeting and talking out of turn?\n\nPatient: You know, honestly, no. That’s one area where I never really struggled back then. Making friends was actually pretty easy for me. I think when you’re a kid, especially a boy in elementary or middle school, having a ton of energy and always wanting to do something doesn’t exactly make you a pariah, you know? If anything, it kind of worked in my favor socially. I was always the one ready to go play wall-ball, or organize a game of pickup basketball, or just, you know, goof around in the back of the bus. \n\nThe other kids didn’t care if my leg was bouncing a million miles an hour under the cafeteria table. They definitely didn't care if I talked out of turn in class—honestly, half the time they probably thought it was funny when I blurted something out and derailed the teacher's lesson. It made me a bit of a class clown, I guess, or at least the guy who kept things from getting boring. My parents and teachers, they never once pulled me aside to say, 'Hey, your fidgeting is alienating your peers' or 'You need to calm down so kids will like you.' Their whole focus, one hundred percent of the time, was just on getting me to sit still long enough to finish a math worksheet. \n\nSo socially, I always had a really solid group of guys I hung out with. We’d ride our bikes all over the neighborhood, build ramps, run around until the streetlights came on... and my restlessness just sort of blended right into all that childhood chaos. It’s actually kind of ironic when you think about it, right? The exact same behavior that makes you an absolute nightmare for a fourth-grade teacher trying to get through a reading lesson is exactly what makes you a fun kid to hang out with at recess. \n\nIt wasn't until I got older, and especially now in the business world like I was saying before, that the talking over people and the constant moving started actually bothering people and causing friction. Because adults expect you to sit in a boardroom and be perfectly polished. But back then? No, not at all. The other kids never batted an eye at it, and it never cost me any friendships.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you tested well on exams but struggled with homework and daily assignments. Looking back, do you think your attention and restlessness problems actually prevented you from learning material, or was it more that you just didn't complete the work even though you could have understood it if you'd sat down and done it?\n\nPatient: That is a really interesting question. You know, I’ve actually thought about that a lot over the years, especially after I started the business and suddenly had to learn all these complicated things on the fly—like tax codes and supply chain logistics and all that. And looking back at high school... I honestly don't really know for sure. I don't have a clear-cut answer for you, because I don't really know what it feels like to sit down and deeply study something, so I don't know what I actually missed out on. \n\nI mean, I never felt stupid, you know? I never sat in a class and thought, \"Wow, this material is just way over my head, I can't grasp these concepts.\" If a teacher was standing up at the chalkboard explaining history or science, I was absorbing it. I might have been completely dismantling a clicky pen at my desk, or tapping my foot so hard the kid next to me was getting annoyed, or staring out the window at the parking lot, but I was still hearing the words. And that’s exactly how I could pass those multiple-choice tests I mentioned. I would just sort of recall what Mr. Davis said on a Tuesday while I was taking apart my pen. \n\nBut as far as sitting down with the homework to see if I truly understood it? It’s like... I couldn't even get far enough into the process to find out if I could learn it that way. Doing the homework was just a complete non-starter. My mom, bless her, she used to try everything. She would set this little plastic tomato kitchen timer and say, \"Just sit here at the table for twenty minutes. Just twenty minutes, read the biology chapter, and then you can go outside.\" And I would sit there, and I would stare at the page, and I could physically read the words, but my brain was already in the backyard, or thinking about building a bike ramp, or just literally buzzing with this overwhelming physical need to stand up. It was like the bridge between my brain understanding a concept and my hand actually writing it down on a worksheet was just completely broken. \n\nSo, did the restlessness prevent me from learning the material? Maybe? I guess it depends on how you define learning. I probably missed out on a lot of the deeper details because I never did the reading. But I always felt like, if I could have just magically downloaded the information into my head while I was walking around or doing something active, I would have understood it perfectly fine. Even today, if my lawyers hand me a fifty-page contract to review, I can't just sit in my leather chair and read it line by line. I’ll pace the entire length of the office, or I'll have my assistant break it down into bullet points and read them out loud to me while I'm pacing, because if I try to just sit still and read it, my eyes glaze over, my leg starts bouncing, and I am just... I'm done. The tank is empty. So I guess, to answer your question, I really don't know if I could have learned it better back then. I just know that the physical act of sitting still to do the daily work was basically impossible for me, regardless of whether I understood the subject or not.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you dropped out of high school and that you've always struggled with sitting still and focusing. But before you were 12 years old—so in elementary school—did your teachers or parents ever say that your attention or restlessness problems were actually getting in the way of you learning or doing well in school, or was it more just that they found you annoying to deal with?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, before I was twelve... so we're talking elementary school, right? First through fifth grade. Yeah, I think it was definitely way more on the \"annoying to deal with\" side of things, honestly. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about my grades and the testing, they weren't really worried that I wasn't grasping the concepts, because I could spit the answers right back at them. The problem was *how* I was spitting the answers back. Looking back, this was definitely around when I was a kid too—teachers were just always, constantly moving my seat. It was like this never-ending game of musical chairs, but I was the only one playing. \n\nI’d start the year in the middle row, right? But then I talked too much to whoever was sitting next to me, so the teacher would move me to the front corner to keep an eye on me. But then I’d be right in their line of sight, and I was always getting up without asking—just standing up and wandering over to the pencil sharpener, or going to look out the window, or walking over to check on the class hamster right in the middle of a spelling lesson. So then they’d get fed up with me pacing around the front of the room and they'd move my desk to the very back. \n\nBut putting me in the back didn't stop me, because then I'd just be blurting things out before they even finished the question. A teacher would be halfway through setting up a math problem on the chalkboard and I'd just shout out \"seventy-two!\" from the back row. I just couldn't hold it in. My brain was moving fast, I knew the answer, so why wait for her to finish talking, you know? \n\nSo when it came to parent-teacher conferences, it was never really about my learning or my academic potential. My mom would come home, sit at the kitchen table, let out this huge sigh, and tell me the teacher just spent twenty minutes complaining about how I couldn't stay in my chair and how I wouldn't stop talking over people. They were just exasperated. I think they just found me exhausting to manage. The whole focus was just trying to figure out where to put my desk so I'd stop driving everyone crazy, rather than worrying about whether I was actually absorbing the geography lesson."
  }
